========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, I came in because there’s this thing that happens to me, and it’s been bothering me more than I like to admit. If I step into a slow elevator, or sometimes a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it and walk out, my chest just sort of jumps right away. It’s immediate, before I’ve even had time to tell myself anything sensible.

And then I get this strong feeling like I have to get back out. Not in a casual way, but like my whole body is saying, “Get out now.” It’s hard to think straight in that moment. I know it may sound simple, but when it happens, it feels very real, and I don’t like feeling that way at my age.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I don’t know that I’d say it feels exactly like a heart attack or that I’m surely going to pass out. It’s more that it comes on right away, almost the second I’m in there — like if I step into a slow elevator, or I’m in a small room and the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it. My chest sort of jumps, and before I’ve even had a chance to think it through, I get this very strong urge to get back out.

It rises fast, I suppose that’s the best way to say it. It’s not me calmly deciding I don’t like the place. My body reacts first, and then I’m trying to catch up with it. In that moment I’m just thinking, “I need to get out of here,” and it’s hard to think straight until I can.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’d put it that way, no. I mean, in the moment it feels very urgent, like I need to get out right then, and I can get pretty flustered. But “lose control” or “complete breakdown” sounds stronger than what I’ve experienced.

It’s more like I get overwhelmed by the feeling, if that makes sense. I’m not thinking clearly the way I normally would, but I don’t think I’ve ever felt like I was going to go crazy or completely fall apart. Once I’m out, I can usually settle myself down, and then I think, “Well, that was more than it needed to be.” But while I’m in it, it’s hard to reason with myself.

Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It comes on very fast — not after I’ve been sitting there worrying for a long time, but almost as soon as I step into one of those places. I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, I’ve heard people use that phrase, “panic attack,” but I don’t know exactly what counts as one. I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing.

It’s not me calmly deciding I don’t like the place. My body reacts first, and then I’m trying to catch up with it. In that moment I’m just thinking, “I need to get out of here,” and it’s hard to think straight until I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had one of these episodes where you felt like you were going to lose control or have a complete breakdown?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d put it that way, no. I mean, in the moment it feels very urgent, like I need to get out right then, and I can get pretty flustered. But “lose control” or “complete breakdown” sounds stronger than what I’ve experienced.\n\nIt’s more like I get overwhelmed by the feeling, if that makes sense. I’m not thinking clearly the way I normally would, but I don’t think I’ve ever felt like I was going to go crazy or completely fall apart. Once I’m out, I can usually settle myself down, and then I think, “Well, that was more than it needed to be.” But while I’m in it, it’s hard to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes of starting?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It comes on very fast — not after I’ve been sitting there worrying for a long time, but almost as soon as I step into one of those places. Like if it’s a slow elevator, or a little room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it, my chest sort of jumps right away.\n\nAnd then within, I don’t know, very quickly — maybe moments more than minutes — I get that strong need to get back out. It’s like my body has already decided before I’ve even had a chance to think sensibly about it. I’m not calmly thinking, “Oh, this is uncomfortable.” It’s more immediate than that. I just feel, “I need to be out of here,” and it’s hard to think straight until I can leave.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be what people often call panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, I’ve heard people use that phrase, “panic attack,” but I don’t know exactly what counts as one. I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing.\n\nWhat I can say is, when I’m in one of those situations, it does feel sudden and very strong, and I get that feeling of needing to get out. But whether that’s what people mean by a panic attack, I honestly don’t know. I’ve never really thought of it in those terms before. Usually I just think, “I don’t like being in here,” or “I need the door open,” not, “I’m having a panic attack.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. At least, I haven’t noticed it happening that way outside of those kinds of places.

It’s pretty tied to when I step into something like a slow elevator, or a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just get right back out. That’s when my chest jumps right away, almost before I’ve had time to think about it, and I get that strong urge to get out. It’s like my mind catches up after my body has already reacted.

But if I’m just out in a regular open place, or sitting at home, or doing ordinary things, I don’t really have that same sudden intense fear come over me. It’s mostly when I feel closed in like that.

If I step into a slow elevator, or sometimes a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it and walk out, my chest just sort of jumps right away. It comes on very fast — not after I’ve been sitting there worrying for a long time, but almost as soon as I step into one of those places. It’s like my body has already decided before I’ve even had a chance to think sensibly about it. I’m not calmly thinking, “Oh, this is uncomfortable.” It’s more immediate than that. I just feel, “I need to be out of here,” and it’s hard to think straight until I can leave.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be what people often call panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, I’ve heard people use that phrase, “panic attack,” but I don’t know exactly what counts as one. I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing.\n\nWhat I can say is, when I’m in one of those situations, it does feel sudden and very strong, and I get that feeling of needing to get out. But whether that’s what people mean by a panic attack, I honestly don’t know. I’ve never really thought of it in those terms before. Usually I just think, “I don’t like being in here,” or “I need the door open,” not, “I’m having a panic attack.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations that aren't related to being in a small or locked space?\n\nPatient: No, not really. At least, I haven’t noticed it happening that way outside of those kinds of places.\n\nIt’s pretty tied to when I step into something like a slow elevator, or a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just get right back out. That’s when my chest jumps right away, almost before I’ve had time to think about it, and I get that strong urge to get out. It’s like my mind catches up after my body has already reacted.\n\nBut if I’m just out in a regular open place, or sitting at home, or doing ordinary things, I don’t really have that same sudden intense fear come over me. It’s mostly when I feel closed in like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever worry about having one of these episodes before you actually enter a small room or elevator?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d call it worrying ahead of time, exactly. I mean, if I know I’m going to have to use an elevator or go into a small room, I might think, “I hope this isn’t one of those tight-feeling ones,” or “I hope the door opens right away,” but I don’t sit there for hours building it up in my mind.

It’s more like I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. I may notice the elevator before I get in and kind of judge it — like, is it crowded, is it old, does it look slow — but the real strong feeling doesn’t usually hit until I’m actually in there or right at the point where I feel closed in. So before that, it’s more of a cautious feeling than a big episode.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I came in because there’s this thing that happens to me, and it’s been bothering me more than I like to admit. If I step into a slow elevator, or sometimes a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it and walk out, my chest just sort of jumps right away. It’s immediate, before I’ve even had time to tell myself anything sensible.\n\nAnd then I get this strong feeling like I have to get back out. Not in a casual way, but like my whole body is saying, “Get out now.” It’s hard to think straight in that moment. I know it may sound simple, but when it happens, it feels very real, and I don’t like feeling that way at my age.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of needing to get out ever reach a peak where you feel like you're having a heart attack or are going to pass out?\n\nPatient: Well, I don’t know that I’d say it feels exactly like a heart attack or that I’m surely going to pass out. It’s more that it comes on right away, almost the second I’m in there — like if I step into a slow elevator, or I’m in a small room and the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it. My chest sort of jumps, and before I’ve even had a chance to think it through, I get this very strong urge to get back out.\n\nIt rises fast, I suppose that’s the best way to say it. It’s not me calmly deciding I don’t like the place. My body reacts first, and then I’m trying to catch up with it. In that moment I’m just thinking, “I need to get out of here,” and it’s hard to think straight until I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had one of these episodes where you felt like you were going to lose control or have a complete breakdown?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d put it that way, no. I mean, in the moment it feels very urgent, like I need to get out right then, and I can get pretty flustered. But “lose control” or “complete breakdown” sounds stronger than what I’ve experienced.\n\nIt’s more like I get overwhelmed by the feeling, if that makes sense. I’m not thinking clearly the way I normally would, but I don’t think I’ve ever felt like I was going to go crazy or completely fall apart. Once I’m out, I can usually settle myself down, and then I think, “Well, that was more than it needed to be.” But while I’m in it, it’s hard to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes of starting?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It comes on very fast — not after I’ve been sitting there worrying for a long time, but almost as soon as I step into one of those places. Like if it’s a slow elevator, or a little room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it, my chest sort of jumps right away.\n\nAnd then within, I don’t know, very quickly — maybe moments more than minutes — I get that strong need to get back out. It’s like my body has already decided before I’ve even had a chance to think sensibly about it. I’m not calmly thinking, “Oh, this is uncomfortable.” It’s more immediate than that. I just feel, “I need to be out of here,” and it’s hard to think straight until I can leave.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be what people often call panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, I’ve heard people use that phrase, “panic attack,” but I don’t know exactly what counts as one. I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing.\n\nWhat I can say is, when I’m in one of those situations, it does feel sudden and very strong, and I get that feeling of needing to get out. But whether that’s what people mean by a panic attack, I honestly don’t know. I’ve never really thought of it in those terms before. Usually I just think, “I don’t like being in here,” or “I need the door open,” not, “I’m having a panic attack.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations that aren't related to being in a small or locked space?\n\nPatient: No, not really. At least, I haven’t noticed it happening that way outside of those kinds of places.\n\nIt’s pretty tied to when I step into something like a slow elevator, or a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just get right back out. That’s when my chest jumps right away, almost before I’ve had time to think about it, and I get that strong urge to get out. It’s like my mind catches up after my body has already reacted.\n\nBut if I’m just out in a regular open place, or sitting at home, or doing ordinary things, I don’t really have that same sudden intense fear come over me. It’s mostly when I feel closed in like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about having one of these episodes before you actually enter a small room or elevator?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it worrying ahead of time, exactly. I mean, if I know I’m going to have to use an elevator or go into a small room, I might think, “I hope this isn’t one of those tight-feeling ones,” or “I hope the door opens right away,” but I don’t sit there for hours building it up in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. I may notice the elevator before I get in and kind of judge it — like, is it crowded, is it old, does it look slow — but the real strong feeling doesn’t usually hit until I’m actually in there or right at the point where I feel closed in. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the moment. I just sort of make little adjustments.

Like these days, if there’s an elevator and the stairs are manageable, I’ll usually take the stairs. I tell myself it’s good exercise, and at my age I probably need it, but if I’m honest, part of it is not wanting to get stuck in that closed-in feeling. And at the museum, sometimes there are archive jobs or things in tighter back rooms, and I’ll pass on those if I can. I feel a bit foolish saying that, because it sounds silly when I say it out loud, but I’d rather not put myself in that position.

If I can’t really avoid it, I’ll do things like ask a friend to wait nearby, or I’ll make sure somebody knows I’m there. Not in a dramatic way, just… it helps me feel like I’m not completely trapped if the door closes or if it takes too long. I know it probably looks unnecessary to other people, and I do feel a little embarrassed about it, but yes, I guess I do arrange things around it sometimes.

If I step into a slow elevator, or sometimes a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it and walk out, my chest just sort of jumps right away. It’s immediate, before I’ve even had time to tell myself anything sensible.\n\nAnd then I get this strong feeling like I have to get back out. Not in a casual way, but like my whole body is saying, “Get out now.” It’s hard to think straight in that moment. I know it may sound simple, but when it happens, it feels very real, and I don’t like feeling that way at my age.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of needing to get out ever reach a peak where you feel like you're having a heart attack or are going to pass out?\n\nPatient: Well, I don’t know that I’d say it feels exactly like a heart attack or that I’m surely going to pass out. It’s more that it comes on right away, almost the second I’m in there — like if I step into a slow elevator, or I’m in a small room and the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it. My chest sort of jumps, and before I’ve even had a chance to think it through, I get this very strong urge to get back out.\n\nIt rises fast, I suppose that’s the best way to say it. It’s not me calmly deciding I don’t like the place. My body reacts first, and then I’m trying to catch up with it. In that moment I’m just thinking, “I need to get out of here,” and it’s hard to think straight until I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had one of these episodes where you felt like you were going to lose control or have a complete breakdown?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d put it that way, no. I mean, in the moment it feels very urgent, like I need to get out right then, and I can get pretty flustered. But “lose control” or “complete breakdown” sounds stronger than what I’ve experienced.\n\nIt’s more like I get overwhelmed by the feeling, if that makes sense. I’m not thinking clearly the way I normally would, but I don’t think I’ve ever felt like I was going to go crazy or completely fall apart. Once I’m out, I can usually settle myself down, and then I think, “Well, that was more than it needed to be.” But while I’m in it, it’s hard to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes of starting?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It comes on very fast — not after I’ve been sitting there worrying for a long time, but almost as soon as I step into one of those places. Like if it’s a slow elevator, or a little room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it, my chest sort of jumps right away.\n\nAnd then within, I don’t know, very quickly — maybe moments more than minutes — I get that strong need to get back out. But whether that’s what people mean by a panic attack, I honestly don’t know. I’ve never really thought of it in those terms before. It’s mostly when I feel closed in like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about having one of these episodes before you actually enter a small room or elevator?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it worrying ahead of time, exactly. I may notice the elevator before I get in and kind of judge it — like, is it crowded, is it old, does it look slow — but the real strong feeling doesn’t usually hit until I’m actually in there or right at the point where I feel closed in. So before that, it’s more of a cautious feeling than a big episode.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the moment. I know it probably looks unnecessary to other people, and I do feel a little embarrassed about it, but yes, I guess I do arrange things around it sometimes.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you fear having one of these episodes because you're worried about what might happen to you during the episode itself?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Specific Phobia, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations that aren't related to being in a small or locked space?\n\nPatient: No, not really. At least, I haven’t noticed it happening that way outside of those kinds of places.\n\nIt’s pretty tied to when I step into something like a slow elevator, or a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just get right back out. That’s when my chest jumps right away, almost before I’ve had time to think about it, and I get that strong urge to get out. It’s like my mind catches up after my body has already reacted.\n\nBut if I’m just out in a regular open place, or sitting at home, or doing ordinary things, I don’t really have that same sudden intense fear come over me. It’s mostly when I feel closed in like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about having one of these episodes before you actually enter a small room or elevator?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it worrying ahead of time, exactly. I mean, if I know I’m going to have to use an elevator or go into a small room, I might think, “I hope this isn’t one of those tight-feeling ones,” or “I hope the door opens right away,” but I don’t sit there for hours building it up in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. I may notice the elevator before I get in and kind of judge it — like, is it crowded, is it old, does it look slow — but the real strong feeling doesn’t usually hit until I’m actually in there or right at the point where I feel closed in. So before that, it’s more of a cautious feeling than a big episode.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the moment. I just sort of make little adjustments.\n\nLike these days, if there’s an elevator and the stairs are manageable, I’ll usually take the stairs. I tell myself it’s good exercise, and at my age I probably need it, but if I’m honest, part of it is not wanting to get stuck in that closed-in feeling. And at the museum, sometimes there are archive jobs or things in tighter back rooms, and I’ll pass on those if I can. I feel a bit foolish saying that, because it sounds silly when I say it out loud, but I’d rather not put myself in that position.\n\nIf I can’t really avoid it, I’ll do things like ask a friend to wait nearby, or I’ll make sure somebody knows I’m there. Not in a dramatic way, just… it helps me feel like I’m not completely trapped if the door closes or if it takes too long. I know it probably looks unnecessary to other people, and I do feel a little embarrassed about it, but yes, I guess I do arrange things around it sometimes.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you fear having one of these episodes because you're worried about what might happen to you during the episode itself?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 79 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “Something terrible is going to happen to me because of the feeling itself,” if that’s what you mean.

It’s more that once I’m in that situation, I don’t like the feeling of not being able to get out. I may think, “What if this door doesn’t open right away?” or “What if I’m stuck here longer than I can stand?” But I don’t know that I’m afraid of the episode itself doing something to me. It’s more the situation and that closed-in feeling that bothers me.

I guess I’m afraid of being trapped with it, rather than afraid that the fear itself is going to hurt me. Does that make sense?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F40.230\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I came in because there’s this thing that happens to me, and it’s been bothering me more than I like to admit. If I step into a slow elevator, or sometimes a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it and walk out, my chest just sort of jumps right away. It’s immediate, before I’ve even had time to tell myself anything sensible.\n\nAnd then I get this strong feeling like I have to get back out. Not in a casual way, but like my whole body is saying, “Get out now.” It’s hard to think straight in that moment. I know it may sound simple, but when it happens, it feels very real, and I don’t like feeling that way at my age.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of needing to get out ever reach a peak where you feel like you're having a heart attack or are going to pass out?\n\nPatient: Well, I don’t know that I’d say it feels exactly like a heart attack or that I’m surely going to pass out. It’s more that it comes on right away, almost the second I’m in there — like if I step into a slow elevator, or I’m in a small room and the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it. My chest sort of jumps, and before I’ve even had a chance to think it through, I get this very strong urge to get back out.\n\nIt rises fast, I suppose that’s the best way to say it. It’s not me calmly deciding I don’t like the place. My body reacts first, and then I’m trying to catch up with it. In that moment I’m just thinking, “I need to get out of here,” and it’s hard to think straight until I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had one of these episodes where you felt like you were going to lose control or have a complete breakdown?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d put it that way, no. I mean, in the moment it feels very urgent, like I need to get out right then, and I can get pretty flustered. But “lose control” or “complete breakdown” sounds stronger than what I’ve experienced.\n\nIt’s more like I get overwhelmed by the feeling, if that makes sense. I’m not thinking clearly the way I normally would, but I don’t think I’ve ever felt like I was going to go crazy or completely fall apart. Once I’m out, I can usually settle myself down, and then I think, “Well, that was more than it needed to be.” But while I’m in it, it’s hard to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes of starting?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It comes on very fast — not after I’ve been sitting there worrying for a long time, but almost as soon as I step into one of those places. Like if it’s a slow elevator, or a little room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it, my chest sort of jumps right away.\n\nAnd then within, I don’t know, very quickly — maybe moments more than minutes — I get that strong need to get back out. It’s like my body has already decided before I’ve even had a chance to think sensibly about it. I’m not calmly thinking, “Oh, this is uncomfortable.” It’s more immediate than that. I just feel, “I need to be out of here,” and it’s hard to think straight until I can leave.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be what people often call panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, I’ve heard people use that phrase, “panic attack,” but I don’t know exactly what counts as one. I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing.\n\nWhat I can say is, when I’m in one of those situations, it does feel sudden and very strong, and I get that feeling of needing to get out. But whether that’s what people mean by a panic attack, I honestly don’t know. I’ve never really thought of it in those terms before. Usually I just think, “I don’t like being in here,” or “I need the door open,” not, “I’m having a panic attack.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations that aren't related to being in a small or locked space?\n\nPatient: No, not really. At least, I haven’t noticed it happening that way outside of those kinds of places.\n\nIt’s pretty tied to when I step into something like a slow elevator, or a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just get right back out. That’s when my chest jumps right away, almost before I’ve had time to think about it, and I get that strong urge to get out. It’s like my mind catches up after my body has already reacted.\n\nBut if I’m just out in a regular open place, or sitting at home, or doing ordinary things, I don’t really have that same sudden intense fear come over me. It’s mostly when I feel closed in like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about having one of these episodes before you actually enter a small room or elevator?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it worrying ahead of time, exactly. I mean, if I know I’m going to have to use an elevator or go into a small room, I might think, “I hope this isn’t one of those tight-feeling ones,” or “I hope the door opens right away,” but I don’t sit there for hours building it up in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. I may notice the elevator before I get in and kind of judge it — like, is it crowded, is it old, does it look slow — but the real strong feeling doesn’t usually hit until I’m actually in there or right at the point where I feel closed in. So before that, it’s more of a cautious feeling than a big episode.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the moment. I just sort of make little adjustments.\n\nLike these days, if there’s an elevator and the stairs are manageable, I’ll usually take the stairs. I tell myself it’s good exercise, and at my age I probably need it, but if I’m honest, part of it is not wanting to get stuck in that closed-in feeling. And at the museum, sometimes there are archive jobs or things in tighter back rooms, and I’ll pass on those if I can. I feel a bit foolish saying that, because it sounds silly when I say it out loud, but I’d rather not put myself in that position.\n\nIf I can’t really avoid it, I’ll do things like ask a friend to wait nearby, or I’ll make sure somebody knows I’m there. Not in a dramatic way, just… it helps me feel like I’m not completely trapped if the door closes or if it takes too long. I know it probably looks unnecessary to other people, and I do feel a little embarrassed about it, but yes, I guess I do arrange things around it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having one of these episodes because you're worried about what might happen to you during the episode itself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “Something terrible is going to happen to me because of the feeling itself,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that once I’m in that situation, I don’t like the feeling of not being able to get out. It’s more the situation and that closed-in feeling that bothers me.\n\nI guess I’m afraid of being trapped with it, rather than afraid that the fear itself is going to hurt me. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I tell myself it’s good exercise, and at my age I probably need it, but if I’m honest, part of it is not wanting to get stuck in that closed-in feeling. And at the museum, sometimes there are archive jobs or things in tighter back rooms, and I’ll pass on those if I can. I feel a bit foolish saying that, because it sounds silly when I say it out loud, but I’d rather not put myself in that position.\n\nIf I can’t really avoid it, I’ll do things like ask a friend to wait nearby, or I’ll make sure somebody knows I’m there. Not in a dramatic way, just… it helps me feel like I’m not completely trapped if the door closes or if it takes too long. I know it probably looks unnecessary to other people, and I do feel a little embarrassed about it, but yes, I guess I do arrange things around it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having one of these episodes because you're worried about what might happen to you during the episode itself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “Something terrible is going to happen to me because of the feeling itself,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that once I’m in that situation, I don’t like the feeling of not being able to get out. I may think, “What if this door doesn’t open right away?” or “What if I’m stuck here longer than I can stand?” But I don’t know that I’m afraid of the episode itself doing something to me. It’s more the situation and that closed-in feeling that bothers me.\n\nI guess I’m afraid of being trapped with it, rather than afraid that the fear itself is going to hurt me. Does that make sense?\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you afraid of having one of these episodes because you're worried about the panic feeling itself?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 10\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The question is about fear of the panic feeling itself or concern about what the episode might do, which is not covered by the specific phobia symptom profile provided."}

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Specific Phobia, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about having one of these episodes before you actually enter a small room or elevator?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it worrying ahead of time, exactly. I mean, if I know I’m going to have to use an elevator or go into a small room, I might think, “I hope this isn’t one of those tight-feeling ones,” or “I hope the door opens right away,” but I don’t sit there for hours building it up in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. I may notice the elevator before I get in and kind of judge it — like, is it crowded, is it old, does it look slow — but the real strong feeling doesn’t usually hit until I’m actually in there or right at the point where I feel closed in. So before that, it’s more of a cautious feeling than a big episode.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the moment. I just sort of make little adjustments.\n\nLike these days, if there’s an elevator and the stairs are manageable, I’ll usually take the stairs. I tell myself it’s good exercise, and at my age I probably need it, but if I’m honest, part of it is not wanting to get stuck in that closed-in feeling. And at the museum, sometimes there are archive jobs or things in tighter back rooms, and I’ll pass on those if I can. I feel a bit foolish saying that, because it sounds silly when I say it out loud, but I’d rather not put myself in that position.\n\nIf I can’t really avoid it, I’ll do things like ask a friend to wait nearby, or I’ll make sure somebody knows I’m there. Not in a dramatic way, just… it helps me feel like I’m not completely trapped if the door closes or if it takes too long. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “Something terrible is going to happen to me because of the feeling itself,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that once I’m in that situation, I don’t like the feeling of not being able to get out. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think it’s the panic feeling itself that I’m afraid of. I mean, I don’t like it, of course — nobody likes feeling worked up or uncomfortable — but I don’t sit there thinking the panic is going to harm me or anything like that.

It’s more that the place feels wrong to me when I’m closed in. The tightness of the space, the door being shut, not being sure I can just step out right when I want to… that’s what gets to me. The feeling comes along with it, but I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of the feeling by itself. If I’m at home or somewhere open and I feel a little nervous, that’s not really the same thing.

So, no, I don’t believe it’s worry about the panic itself. It’s more the being stuck, or feeling like I might be stuck, that bothers me.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.230, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I came in because there’s this thing that happens to me, and it’s been bothering me more than I like to admit. If I step into a slow elevator, or sometimes a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it and walk out, my chest just sort of jumps right away. It’s immediate, before I’ve even had time to tell myself anything sensible.\n\nAnd then I get this strong feeling like I have to get back out. Not in a casual way, but like my whole body is saying, “Get out now.” It’s hard to think straight in that moment. I know it may sound simple, but when it happens, it feels very real, and I don’t like feeling that way at my age.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of needing to get out ever reach a peak where you feel like you're having a heart attack or are going to pass out?\n\nPatient: Well, I don’t know that I’d say it feels exactly like a heart attack or that I’m surely going to pass out. It’s more that it comes on right away, almost the second I’m in there — like if I step into a slow elevator, or I’m in a small room and the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it. My chest sort of jumps, and before I’ve even had a chance to think it through, I get this very strong urge to get back out.\n\nIt rises fast, I suppose that’s the best way to say it. It’s not me calmly deciding I don’t like the place. My body reacts first, and then I’m trying to catch up with it. In that moment I’m just thinking, “I need to get out of here,” and it’s hard to think straight until I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had one of these episodes where you felt like you were going to lose control or have a complete breakdown?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d put it that way, no. I mean, in the moment it feels very urgent, like I need to get out right then, and I can get pretty flustered. But “lose control” or “complete breakdown” sounds stronger than what I’ve experienced.\n\nIt’s more like I get overwhelmed by the feeling, if that makes sense. I’m not thinking clearly the way I normally would, but I don’t think I’ve ever felt like I was going to go crazy or completely fall apart. Once I’m out, I can usually settle myself down, and then I think, “Well, that was more than it needed to be.” But while I’m in it, it’s hard to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes of starting?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It comes on very fast — not after I’ve been sitting there worrying for a long time, but almost as soon as I step into one of those places. Like if it’s a slow elevator, or a little room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it, my chest sort of jumps right away.\n\nAnd then within, I don’t know, very quickly — maybe moments more than minutes — I get that strong need to get back out. It’s like my body has already decided before I’ve even had a chance to think sensibly about it. I’m not calmly thinking, “Oh, this is uncomfortable.” It’s more immediate than that. I just feel, “I need to be out of here,” and it’s hard to think straight until I can leave.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be what people often call panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, I’ve heard people use that phrase, “panic attack,” but I don’t know exactly what counts as one. I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing.\n\nWhat I can say is, when I’m in one of those situations, it does feel sudden and very strong, and I get that feeling of needing to get out. But whether that’s what people mean by a panic attack, I honestly don’t know. I’ve never really thought of it in those terms before. Usually I just think, “I don’t like being in here,” or “I need the door open,” not, “I’m having a panic attack.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations that aren't related to being in a small or locked space?\n\nPatient: No, not really. At least, I haven’t noticed it happening that way outside of those kinds of places.\n\nIt’s pretty tied to when I step into something like a slow elevator, or a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just get right back out. That’s when my chest jumps right away, almost before I’ve had time to think about it, and I get that strong urge to get out. It’s like my mind catches up after my body has already reacted.\n\nBut if I’m just out in a regular open place, or sitting at home, or doing ordinary things, I don’t really have that same sudden intense fear come over me. It’s mostly when I feel closed in like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about having one of these episodes before you actually enter a small room or elevator?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it worrying ahead of time, exactly. I mean, if I know I’m going to have to use an elevator or go into a small room, I might think, “I hope this isn’t one of those tight-feeling ones,” or “I hope the door opens right away,” but I don’t sit there for hours building it up in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. I may notice the elevator before I get in and kind of judge it — like, is it crowded, is it old, does it look slow — but the real strong feeling doesn’t usually hit until I’m actually in there or right at the point where I feel closed in. So before that, it’s more of a cautious feeling than a big episode.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the moment. I just sort of make little adjustments.\n\nLike these days, if there’s an elevator and the stairs are manageable, I’ll usually take the stairs. I tell myself it’s good exercise, and at my age I probably need it, but if I’m honest, part of it is not wanting to get stuck in that closed-in feeling. And at the museum, sometimes there are archive jobs or things in tighter back rooms, and I’ll pass on those if I can. I feel a bit foolish saying that, because it sounds silly when I say it out loud, but I’d rather not put myself in that position.\n\nIf I can’t really avoid it, I’ll do things like ask a friend to wait nearby, or I’ll make sure somebody knows I’m there. Not in a dramatic way, just… it helps me feel like I’m not completely trapped if the door closes or if it takes too long. I know it probably looks unnecessary to other people, and I do feel a little embarrassed about it, but yes, I guess I do arrange things around it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having one of these episodes because you're worried about what might happen to you during the episode itself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “Something terrible is going to happen to me because of the feeling itself,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that once I’m in that situation, I don’t like the feeling of not being able to get out. I may think, “What if this door doesn’t open right away?” or “What if I’m stuck here longer than I can stand?” But I don’t know that I’m afraid of the episode itself doing something to me. It’s more the situation and that closed-in feeling that bothers me.\n\nI guess I’m afraid of being trapped with it, rather than afraid that the fear itself is going to hurt me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of having one of these episodes because you're worried about the panic feeling itself?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s the panic feeling itself that I’m afraid of. I mean, I don’t like it, of course — nobody likes feeling worked up or uncomfortable — but I don’t sit there thinking the panic is going to harm me or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that the place feels wrong to me when I’m closed in. The tightness of the space, the door being shut, not being sure I can just step out right when I want to… that’s what gets to me. The feeling comes along with it, but I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of the feeling by itself. If I’m at home or somewhere open and I feel a little nervous, that’s not really the same thing.\n\nSo, no, I don’t believe it’s worry about the panic itself. It’s more the being stuck, or feeling like I might be stuck, that bothers me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Specific Phobia. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).\n  manifestation: I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up, but my mind treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight.\n- key: S063\n  name: Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response\n  description: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that whenever I step into a slow elevator or a small locked room, my chest jumps right away and I get this strong need to get back out before I can even think straight.\n- key: S064\n  name: Phobic_Avoidance_Endure\n  description: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.\n  manifestation: These days I take the stairs, pass on archive jobs at the museum, or ask a friend to wait nearby if I can't avoid a closed-in place, even though I feel a bit foolish doing it.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I've had to turn down some museum docent tasks and skip a few gallery events because I don't want to get stuck dealing with those closed-in rooms or elevators. It hasn't stopped my whole life, but it has made my volunteering and outings more limited than they used to be.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since that brief detention after the housing-rights demonstration, about a year and a month ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 79 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S063\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the moment. I just sort of make little adjustments.\n\nLike these days, if there’s an elevator and the stairs are manageable, I’ll usually take the stairs. I tell myself it’s good exercise, and at my age I probably need it, but if I’m honest, part of it is not wanting to get stuck in that closed-in feeling. And at the museum, sometimes there are archive jobs or things in tighter back rooms, and I’ll pass on those if I can. I feel a bit foolish saying that, because it sounds silly when I say it out loud, but I’d rather not put myself in that position.\n\nIf I can’t really avoid it, I’ll do things like ask a friend to wait nearby, or I’ll make sure somebody knows I’m there. Not in a dramatic way, just… it helps me feel like I’m not completely trapped if the door closes or if it takes too long. I know it probably looks unnecessary to other people, and I do feel a little embarrassed about it, but yes, I guess I do arrange things around it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having one of these episodes because you're worried about what might happen to you during the episode itself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “Something terrible is going to happen to me because of the feeling itself,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that once I’m in that situation, I don’t like the feeling of not being able to get out. I may think, “What if this door doesn’t open right away?” or “What if I’m stuck here longer than I can stand?” But I don’t know that I’m afraid of the episode itself doing something to me. It’s more the situation and that closed-in feeling that bothers me.\n\nI guess I’m afraid of being trapped with it, rather than afraid that the fear itself is going to hurt me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of having one of these episodes because you're worried about the panic feeling itself?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s the panic feeling itself that I’m afraid of. I mean, I don’t like it, of course — nobody likes feeling worked up or uncomfortable — but I don’t sit there thinking the panic is going to harm me or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that the place feels wrong to me when I’m closed in. The tightness of the space, the door being shut, not being sure I can just step out right when I want to… that’s what gets to me. The feeling comes along with it, but I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of the feeling by itself. If I’m at home or somewhere open and I feel a little nervous, that’s not really the same thing.\n\nSo, no, I don’t believe it’s worry about the panic itself. It’s more the being stuck, or feeling like I might be stuck, that bothers me.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your fear more about being in the enclosed space itself or about the possibility of having one of these episodes while you're in it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more about the enclosed space itself, I think. The episode, or the worked-up feeling, comes after I’m already in there and my mind has decided, “This isn’t good.”

Like, I know perfectly well that a little storage room at the museum, or an elevator, isn’t the same thing as being locked up somewhere. I can say that sitting here calmly. But when I see there’s no window, and the door shuts tight, it’s like my mind treats it as trouble right away. Before I’ve even had time to reason with myself, I’m already noticing how close the walls feel and whether I can get back out easily.

So no, I don’t think I’m mostly worrying, “What if I have an episode in here?” It’s more that the closed-in place sets the whole thing off. The tight door, no window, no easy way to step out — that’s what bothers me.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.230, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I came in because there’s this thing that happens to me, and it’s been bothering me more than I like to admit. If I step into a slow elevator, or sometimes a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it and walk out, my chest just sort of jumps right away. It’s immediate, before I’ve even had time to tell myself anything sensible.\n\nAnd then I get this strong feeling like I have to get back out. Not in a casual way, but like my whole body is saying, “Get out now.” It’s hard to think straight in that moment. I know it may sound simple, but when it happens, it feels very real, and I don’t like feeling that way at my age.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of needing to get out ever reach a peak where you feel like you're having a heart attack or are going to pass out?\n\nPatient: Well, I don’t know that I’d say it feels exactly like a heart attack or that I’m surely going to pass out. It’s more that it comes on right away, almost the second I’m in there — like if I step into a slow elevator, or I’m in a small room and the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it. My chest sort of jumps, and before I’ve even had a chance to think it through, I get this very strong urge to get back out.\n\nIt rises fast, I suppose that’s the best way to say it. It’s not me calmly deciding I don’t like the place. My body reacts first, and then I’m trying to catch up with it. In that moment I’m just thinking, “I need to get out of here,” and it’s hard to think straight until I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had one of these episodes where you felt like you were going to lose control or have a complete breakdown?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d put it that way, no. I mean, in the moment it feels very urgent, like I need to get out right then, and I can get pretty flustered. But “lose control” or “complete breakdown” sounds stronger than what I’ve experienced.\n\nIt’s more like I get overwhelmed by the feeling, if that makes sense. I’m not thinking clearly the way I normally would, but I don’t think I’ve ever felt like I was going to go crazy or completely fall apart. Once I’m out, I can usually settle myself down, and then I think, “Well, that was more than it needed to be.” But while I’m in it, it’s hard to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes of starting?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It comes on very fast — not after I’ve been sitting there worrying for a long time, but almost as soon as I step into one of those places. Like if it’s a slow elevator, or a little room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it, my chest sort of jumps right away.\n\nAnd then within, I don’t know, very quickly — maybe moments more than minutes — I get that strong need to get back out. It’s like my body has already decided before I’ve even had a chance to think sensibly about it. I’m not calmly thinking, “Oh, this is uncomfortable.” It’s more immediate than that. I just feel, “I need to be out of here,” and it’s hard to think straight until I can leave.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be what people often call panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, I’ve heard people use that phrase, “panic attack,” but I don’t know exactly what counts as one. I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing.\n\nWhat I can say is, when I’m in one of those situations, it does feel sudden and very strong, and I get that feeling of needing to get out. But whether that’s what people mean by a panic attack, I honestly don’t know. I’ve never really thought of it in those terms before. Usually I just think, “I don’t like being in here,” or “I need the door open,” not, “I’m having a panic attack.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations that aren't related to being in a small or locked space?\n\nPatient: No, not really. At least, I haven’t noticed it happening that way outside of those kinds of places.\n\nIt’s pretty tied to when I step into something like a slow elevator, or a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just get right back out. That’s when my chest jumps right away, almost before I’ve had time to think about it, and I get that strong urge to get out. It’s like my mind catches up after my body has already reacted.\n\nBut if I’m just out in a regular open place, or sitting at home, or doing ordinary things, I don’t really have that same sudden intense fear come over me. It’s mostly when I feel closed in like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about having one of these episodes before you actually enter a small room or elevator?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it worrying ahead of time, exactly. I mean, if I know I’m going to have to use an elevator or go into a small room, I might think, “I hope this isn’t one of those tight-feeling ones,” or “I hope the door opens right away,” but I don’t sit there for hours building it up in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. I may notice the elevator before I get in and kind of judge it — like, is it crowded, is it old, does it look slow — but the real strong feeling doesn’t usually hit until I’m actually in there or right at the point where I feel closed in. So before that, it’s more of a cautious feeling than a big episode.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the moment. I just sort of make little adjustments.\n\nLike these days, if there’s an elevator and the stairs are manageable, I’ll usually take the stairs. I tell myself it’s good exercise, and at my age I probably need it, but if I’m honest, part of it is not wanting to get stuck in that closed-in feeling. And at the museum, sometimes there are archive jobs or things in tighter back rooms, and I’ll pass on those if I can. I feel a bit foolish saying that, because it sounds silly when I say it out loud, but I’d rather not put myself in that position.\n\nIf I can’t really avoid it, I’ll do things like ask a friend to wait nearby, or I’ll make sure somebody knows I’m there. Not in a dramatic way, just… it helps me feel like I’m not completely trapped if the door closes or if it takes too long. I know it probably looks unnecessary to other people, and I do feel a little embarrassed about it, but yes, I guess I do arrange things around it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having one of these episodes because you're worried about what might happen to you during the episode itself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “Something terrible is going to happen to me because of the feeling itself,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that once I’m in that situation, I don’t like the feeling of not being able to get out. I may think, “What if this door doesn’t open right away?” or “What if I’m stuck here longer than I can stand?” But I don’t know that I’m afraid of the episode itself doing something to me. It’s more the situation and that closed-in feeling that bothers me.\n\nI guess I’m afraid of being trapped with it, rather than afraid that the fear itself is going to hurt me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of having one of these episodes because you're worried about the panic feeling itself?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s the panic feeling itself that I’m afraid of. I mean, I don’t like it, of course — nobody likes feeling worked up or uncomfortable — but I don’t sit there thinking the panic is going to harm me or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that the place feels wrong to me when I’m closed in. The tightness of the space, the door being shut, not being sure I can just step out right when I want to… that’s what gets to me. The feeling comes along with it, but I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of the feeling by itself. If I’m at home or somewhere open and I feel a little nervous, that’s not really the same thing.\n\nSo, no, I don’t believe it’s worry about the panic itself. It’s more the being stuck, or feeling like I might be stuck, that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear more about being in the enclosed space itself or about the possibility of having one of these episodes while you're in it?\n\nPatient: It’s more about the enclosed space itself, I think. The episode, or the worked-up feeling, comes after I’m already in there and my mind has decided, “This isn’t good.”\n\nLike, I know perfectly well that a little storage room at the museum, or an elevator, isn’t the same thing as being locked up somewhere. I can say that sitting here calmly. But when I see there’s no window, and the door shuts tight, it’s like my mind treats it as trouble right away. Before I’ve even had time to reason with myself, I’m already noticing how close the walls feel and whether I can get back out easily.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’m mostly worrying, “What if I have an episode in here?” It’s more that the closed-in place sets the whole thing off. The tight door, no window, no easy way to step out — that’s what bothers me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I may notice the elevator before I get in and kind of judge it — like, is it crowded, is it old, does it look slow — but the real strong feeling doesn’t usually hit until I’m actually in there or right at the point where I feel closed in. So before that, it’s more of a cautious feeling than a big episode.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the moment. I just sort of make little adjustments.\n\nLike these days, if there’s an elevator and the stairs are manageable, I’ll usually take the stairs. I tell myself it’s good exercise, and at my age I probably need it, but if I’m honest, part of it is not wanting to get stuck in that closed-in feeling. And at the museum, sometimes there are archive jobs or things in tighter back rooms, and I’ll pass on those if I can. I feel a bit foolish saying that, because it sounds silly when I say it out loud, but I’d rather not put myself in that position.\n\nIf I can’t really avoid it, I’ll do things like ask a friend to wait nearby, or I’ll make sure somebody knows I’m there. Not in a dramatic way, just… it helps me feel like I’m not completely trapped if the door closes or if it takes too long. I know it probably looks unnecessary to other people, and I do feel a little embarrassed about it, but yes, I guess I do arrange things around it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having one of these episodes because you're worried about what might happen to you during the episode itself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “Something terrible is going to happen to me because of the feeling itself,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that once I’m in that situation, I don’t like the feeling of not being able to get out. I may think, “What if this door doesn’t open right away?” or “What if I’m stuck here longer than I can stand?” But I don’t know that I’m afraid of the episode itself doing something to me. It’s more the situation and that closed-in feeling that bothers me.\n\nI guess I’m afraid of being trapped with it, rather than afraid that the fear itself is going to hurt me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of having one of these episodes because you're worried about the panic feeling itself?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s the panic feeling itself that I’m afraid of. I mean, I don’t like it, of course — nobody likes feeling worked up or uncomfortable — but I don’t sit there thinking the panic is going to harm me or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that the place feels wrong to me when I’m closed in. The tightness of the space, the door being shut, not being sure I can just step out right when I want to… that’s what gets to me. The feeling comes along with it, but I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of the feeling by itself. If I’m at home or somewhere open and I feel a little nervous, that’s not really the same thing.\n\nSo, no, I don’t believe it’s worry about the panic itself. It’s more the being stuck, or feeling like I might be stuck, that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear more about being in the enclosed space itself or about the possibility of having one of these episodes while you're in it?\n\nPatient: It’s more about the enclosed space itself, I think. The episode, or the worked-up feeling, comes after I’m already in there and my mind has decided, “This isn’t good.”\n\nLike, I know perfectly well that a little storage room at the museum, or an elevator, isn’t the same thing as being locked up somewhere. I can say that sitting here calmly. But when I see there’s no window, and the door shuts tight, it’s like my mind treats it as trouble right away. Before I’ve even had time to reason with myself, I’m already noticing how close the walls feel and whether I can get back out easily.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’m mostly worrying, “What if I have an episode in here?” It’s more that the closed-in place sets the whole thing off. The tight door, no window, no easy way to step out — that’s what bothers me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s the space itself, mostly. I don’t really go into it thinking, “Oh no, I’m going to have a panic attack in here.” It’s more immediate than that.

Like I said, I can know in my head that a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up somewhere. I do understand that. But the minute I see there’s no window, and then the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble. It’s almost like I don’t get a chance to talk myself out of it first.

Then I start focusing on the door and how close everything feels, and whether I could get out right away if I needed to. So the uncomfortable feeling comes from being in that closed-in place. It’s not so much that I’m afraid of having a panic attack there — it’s that the enclosed space sets me off.

If I step into a slow elevator, or sometimes a small room where the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it and walk out, my chest just sort of jumps right away. It’s more that it comes on right away, almost the second I’m in there — like if I step into a slow elevator, or I’m in a small room and the door is locked or feels like I can’t just open it.
