It’s almost like I don’t get a chance to talk myself out of it first.\n\nThen I start focusing on the door and how close everything feels, and whether I could get out right away if I needed to. I know that logically. But when I’m actually there and I see there’s no window, and then the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble right away. It’s not like I’m calmly thinking, “I’m afraid of the panic feeling.” It’s more that the space feels wrong, like I need to be able to get out.\n\nThe panic or nervous feeling comes after that, if that makes sense. It’s the closed-in place that starts it.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience in enclosed spaces primarily about the panic or discomfort you feel, or is it about something else related to the space?\n\nPatient: It’s more about the space, I’d say — not just the panic or the uncomfortable feeling after it starts. I know I probably keep circling back to the same thing, but that’s really how it feels to me.\n\nA little storage room, or an elevator at the museum, I can understand in my head that it’s not the same as being locked up somewhere. I’m not confused about that. But when I’m actually there, and I notice there’s no window, and then the door closes tight, something in my mind just says, “This is trouble.” It happens before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nSo the discomfort comes from that — from the closed-in space, the no window, the door being shut, and feeling like I can’t just step right out. That’s the part my mind reacts to.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of enclosed spaces because you dislike the feeling of being trapped or because you worry about the panic that occurs when you are in those spaces?\n\nPatient: It’s more the trapped feeling, the space itself. Not so much that I’m sitting there worrying, “What if I panic?” That isn’t really the first thing in my head.\n\nI know, when I’m thinking about it calmly, that a small storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up somewhere. I do know that. But the minute I’m in there and I see there’s no window, and then the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble. Like it doesn’t wait for me to reason it out.\n\nSo it’s that closed-in feeling — no window, the door shut, not feeling like I can just step out right away — that gets to me. The panic feeling, or whatever you’d call it, comes from that.\n\nDoctor: Is it the idea of being in a small, enclosed space that scares you, or is it what might happen to you while you're in that space?\n\nPatient: It’s the idea of the small enclosed space, mostly. Or maybe not just the idea of it, but once I’m actually in it and I can see how closed in it is.\n\nI know I’ve said something like this already, but a little storage room or an elevator at the museum — I know those aren’t the same as being locked up somewhere. I do understand that when I’m sitting here talking about it. But when I’m there, and there’s no window, and then the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble right away.\n\nIt’s not really that I’m thinking through all the things that might happen to me in there. It’s more that the place itself feels wrong, like I shouldn’t be in there and I need a way out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of enclosed spaces if you knew you wouldn't have a panic attack in them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I probably would still be afraid, at least some. Because it isn’t only the panic part I’m afraid of.\n\nI mean, if you could somehow promise me, “You won’t have that panicky feeling,” I still think when I stepped into a little storage room or got into an elevator at the museum and saw there was no window, and then the door shut tight, my mind would still react. It’s like it treats that setup itself as trouble.\n\nI know, sitting here, that those places aren’t the same as being locked up somewhere. I do know that. But in the moment, the closed-in part is what catches me first. The no window, the door closed, that feeling that I can’t just step right out — that’s what scares me before I even get to thinking about whether I’ll panic.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of enclosed spaces is the main problem you're dealing with, rather than the panic or discomfort you feel when you're in those spaces?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say that’s right. It’s like my mind catches up after my body has already reacted.\n\nBut if I’m just out in a regular open place, or sitting at home, or doing ordinary things, I don’t really have that same sudden intense fear come over me. It’s mostly when I feel closed in like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about having one of these episodes before you actually enter a small room or elevator?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it worrying ahead of time, exactly. I mean, if I know I’m going to have to use an elevator or go into a small room, I might think, “I hope this isn’t one of those tight-feeling ones,” or “I hope the door opens right away,” but I don’t sit there for hours building it up in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like I’m aware of it, if that makes sense. I may notice the elevator before I get in and kind of judge it — like, is it crowded, is it old, does it look slow — but the real strong feeling doesn’t usually hit until I’m actually in there or right at the point where I feel closed in. So before that, it’s more of a cautious feeling than a big episode.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the moment. I just sort of make little adjustments.\n\nLike these days, if there’s an elevator and the stairs are manageable, I’ll usually take the stairs. I tell myself it’s good exercise, and at my age I probably need it, but if I’m honest, part of it is not wanting to get stuck in that closed-in feeling. And at the museum, sometimes there are archive jobs or things in tighter back rooms, and I’ll pass on those if I can. I feel a bit foolish saying that, because it sounds silly when I say it out loud, but I’d rather not put myself in that position.\n\nIf I can’t really avoid it, I’ll do things like ask a friend to wait nearby, or I’ll make sure somebody knows I’m there. Not in a dramatic way, just… it helps me feel like I’m not completely trapped if the door closes or if it takes too long. I know it probably looks unnecessary to other people, and I do feel a little embarrassed about it, but yes, I guess I do arrange things around it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having one of these episodes because you're worried about what might happen to you during the episode itself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “Something terrible is going to happen to me because of the feeling itself,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that once I’m in that situation, I don’t like the feeling of not being able to get out. I may think, “What if this door doesn’t open right away?” or “What if I’m stuck here longer than I can stand?” But I don’t know that I’m afraid of the episode itself doing something to me. It’s more the situation and that closed-in feeling that bothers me.\n\nI guess I’m afraid of being trapped with it, rather than afraid that the fear itself is going to hurt me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of having one of these episodes because you're worried about the panic feeling itself?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s the panic feeling itself that I’m afraid of. I mean, I don’t like it, of course — nobody likes feeling worked up or uncomfortable — but I don’t sit there thinking the panic is going to harm me or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that the place feels wrong to me when I’m closed in. The tightness of the space, the door being shut, not being sure I can just step out right when I want to… that’s what gets to me. The feeling comes along with it, but I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of the feeling by itself. If I’m at home or somewhere open and I feel a little nervous, that’s not really the same thing.\n\nSo, no, I don’t believe it’s worry about the panic itself. It’s more the being stuck, or feeling like I might be stuck, that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear more about being in the enclosed space itself or about the possibility of having one of these episodes while you're in it?\n\nPatient: It’s more about the enclosed space itself, I think. The episode, or the worked-up feeling, comes after I’m already in there and my mind has decided, “This isn’t good.”\n\nLike, I know perfectly well that a little storage room at the museum, or an elevator, isn’t the same thing as being locked up somewhere. I can say that sitting here calmly. But when I see there’s no window, and the door shuts tight, it’s like my mind treats it as trouble right away. Before I’ve even had time to reason with myself, I’m already noticing how close the walls feel and whether I can get back out easily.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’m mostly worrying, “What if I have an episode in here?” It’s more that the closed-in place sets the whole thing off. The tight door, no window, no easy way to step out — that’s what bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of being in enclosed spaces because of the space itself or because you're afraid of having a panic attack in that space?\n\nPatient: It’s the space itself, mostly. I don’t really go into it thinking, “Oh no, I’m going to have a panic attack in here.” It’s more immediate than that.\n\nLike I said, I can know in my head that a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up somewhere. I do understand that. But the minute I see there’s no window, and then the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble. It’s almost like I don’t get a chance to talk myself out of it first.\n\nThen I start focusing on the door and how close everything feels, and whether I could get out right away if I needed to. So the uncomfortable feeling comes from being in that closed-in place. It’s not so much that I’m afraid of having a panic attack there — it’s that the enclosed space sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern the feeling of panic that arises when you're in an enclosed space, or is it the enclosed space itself that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It’s the enclosed space itself, really. I know I’ve said it a few ways, but that’s the part that bothers me most.\n\nI can tell myself, sitting here, that a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up someplace. I know that logically. But when I’m actually there and I see there’s no window, and then the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble right away. It’s not like I’m calmly thinking, “I’m afraid of the panic feeling.” It’s more that the space feels wrong, like I need to be able to get out.\n\nThe panic or nervous feeling comes after that, if that makes sense. It’s the closed-in place that starts it.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience in enclosed spaces primarily about the panic or discomfort you feel, or is it about something else related to the space?\n\nPatient: It’s more about the space, I’d say — not just the panic or the uncomfortable feeling after it starts. I know I probably keep circling back to the same thing, but that’s really how it feels to me.\n\nA little storage room, or an elevator at the museum, I can understand in my head that it’s not the same as being locked up somewhere. I’m not confused about that. But when I’m actually there, and I notice there’s no window, and then the door closes tight, something in my mind just says, “This is trouble.” It happens before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nSo the discomfort comes from that — from the closed-in space, the no window, the door being shut, and feeling like I can’t just step right out. That’s the part my mind reacts to.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of enclosed spaces because you dislike the feeling of being trapped or because you worry about the panic that occurs when you are in those spaces?\n\nPatient: It’s more the trapped feeling, the space itself. Not so much that I’m sitting there worrying, “What if I panic?” That isn’t really the first thing in my head.\n\nI know, when I’m thinking about it calmly, that a small storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up somewhere. I do know that. But the minute I’m in there and I see there’s no window, and then the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble. Like it doesn’t wait for me to reason it out.\n\nSo it’s that closed-in feeling — no window, the door shut, not feeling like I can just step out right away — that gets to me. The panic feeling, or whatever you’d call it, comes from that.\n\nDoctor: Is it the idea of being in a small, enclosed space that scares you, or is it what might happen to you while you're in that space?\n\nPatient: It’s the idea of the small enclosed space, mostly. Or maybe not just the idea of it, but once I’m actually in it and I can see how closed in it is.\n\nI know I’ve said something like this already, but a little storage room or an elevator at the museum — I know those aren’t the same as being locked up somewhere. I do understand that when I’m sitting here talking about it. But when I’m there, and there’s no window, and then the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble right away.\n\nIt’s not really that I’m thinking through all the things that might happen to me in there. It’s more that the place itself feels wrong, like I shouldn’t be in there and I need a way out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of enclosed spaces if you knew you wouldn't have a panic attack in them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I probably would still be afraid, at least some. Because it isn’t only the panic part I’m afraid of.\n\nI mean, if you could somehow promise me, “You won’t have that panicky feeling,” I still think when I stepped into a little storage room or got into an elevator at the museum and saw there was no window, and then the door shut tight, my mind would still react. It’s like it treats that setup itself as trouble.\n\nI know, sitting here, that those places aren’t the same as being locked up somewhere. I do know that. But in the moment, the closed-in part is what catches me first. The no window, the door closed, that feeling that I can’t just step right out — that’s what scares me before I even get to thinking about whether I’ll panic.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of enclosed spaces is the main problem you're dealing with, rather than the panic or discomfort you feel when you're in those spaces?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say that’s right. The enclosed space itself is the main problem for me.\n\nI know I’m repeating myself a bit, but it’s like… I can understand, sitting here, that a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up somewhere. I’m not confused about that. But when I’m actually there, and I see there’s no window, and then the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble right away.\n\nSo the discomfort comes after that, if that makes sense. The panic or uneasy feeling is there because the space feels closed in and wrong to me. It’s not that I’m mainly worried about the panic itself. It’s more that the room or elevator feels like something I need to get out of.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of enclosed spaces something you think you would still experience even if you were certain you wouldn't have a panic attack in those spaces?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. I know that sounds like I’m going in circles, but it’s not only the panic attack I’m afraid of.\n\nIt’s more like… I can know, sitting here with you, that a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same thing as being locked up. I do know that in my head. But once I’m there, and I see there’s no window, and the door shuts tight, my mind treats it like trouble right away. It’s almost before I’ve had time to think it through.\n\nSo even if you told me for certain, “You won’t have a panic attack,” I still think I’d feel that fear when the space closes in like that. The closed-in part itself is what gets me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of enclosed spaces due to the spaces themselves or because of the panic attacks you experience in them?\n\nPatient: It’s the spaces themselves, I’d say. The panic, or whatever you’d call that awful feeling, can happen after, but the first thing is the place being closed in.\n\nLike I know, sitting here, that a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up somewhere. I understand that. But when I’m actually there and I notice there’s no window, and then the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble right away. It’s like something in me says, “No, this isn’t okay,” before I’ve even had much time to think about it.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s mainly because of the panic attacks. It’s more that the enclosed space itself sets me off."
